). It is immediate from the definition of the modular reduction functor
\mathbb{F}
that the outer square commutes. In the left square, the horizontal arrows are essentially surjective since all
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}(\mu,\mathcal{L})
are in the image, and these generate under direct sums by Proposition
5.3.14
. The maps on morphisms are described in §
5.6
, in terms of Lemma
5.3.1
. From this, we see that the commutativity of the outer square of (
7.18
) implies commutativity of the right square.

Since the right square of (
7.18
) is the same as that of (
7.17
), we have now established that the outer rectangle in (
7.17
) commutes. Therefore, by definition, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{G,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{H,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{T,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{T_{H},S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces br^{(\ell)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CT_{B}[\deg_{G}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CT_{B_{H}}[\deg_{H}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces br^{(\ell)}}

commutes. Finally, applying the Frobenius linearization process of Construction
7.4.1
completes the proof for the commutativity of (
7.16
).
∎

7.6.
The
\sigma
-dual homomorphism

The proof of Theorem
7.4.5
is completed by combining Proposition
7.6.4
, Corollary
7.6.5
, and Proposition
7.6.6
, which are proved below. Before embarking on these proofs, we draw a few consequences.

By the construction of
\widetilde{br}
, Theorem
7.4.5
implies the following:

Theorem 7.6.1
.

Assume
\ell>\max\{b(\check{G}),b(\check{H})\}
. Then the functor
\widetilde{br}\colon\Sat(\Gr_{G,\Div_{X}^{1}};k)\rightarrow\Sat(\Gr_{H,\Div_{X}^{1}};k)
is additive, symmetric monoidal, and compatible with the fiber functor.

Recall that

\Rep_{\Loc(\Div_{X}^{1};\Lambda)}(\check{G})\cong\Rep_{\Rep_{\Lambda}(W_{E})}(\check{G})\cong\Rep_{\Lambda}({}^{L}G)

for the
L
-group

{}^{L}G\cong\check{G}\rtimes W_{E}
. Note that this differs from Langlands’ convention for the
L
-group by a cyclotomic twist on root groups, although the difference can be trivialized by choosing a square root of the cyclotomic character; see
[
FS21
, §VI.11]
for the precise relation.

Corollary 7.6.2
.

Assume
\ell>\max\{b(\check{G}),b(\check{H})\}
. Then the functor

\widetilde{br}\colon\Sat(\Gr_{G,\Div_{X}^{1}};k)\rightarrow\Sat(\Gr_{H,\Div_{X}^{1}};k)

corresponds under the Geometric Satake equivalence to the restriction
{}^{L}\psi\colon\Rep({}^{L}G)\rightarrow\Rep({}^{L}H)
along some homomorphism
{}^{L}\psi\colon{}^{L}H\rightarrow{}^{L}G
.

Proof.

The existence of
{}^{L}\psi
with the stated property follows from the definitions of
{}^{L}G
and
{}^{L}H
via the Tannakian reconstruction process used in
[
FS21
, Proposition VI.10.2]
.

∎

Remark 7.6.3
.

The second main theorem of Treumann-Venkatesh (see
[
TV16
, §1.3]
) is the construction of a
\sigma
-dual homomorphism when
G
is simply connected and
H
is semisimple, with three possible exceptions when
G
has type
\mathrm{E}_{6}
. Their proof is based on classification of all possible examples, and then case-by-case analysis of each. By contrast, our construction is completely uniform.

However, our assumption on
\ell
leaves out many interesting examples in
[
TV16
]
which are specific to small primes. This is partly due to the suboptimal hypotheses in Theorem
6.2.2
, which we believe to be an artefact of the less developed state of geometric representation theory in
p
-adic geometry; for example, we take shortcuts in order to circumvent developing a theory of Soergel bimodules in this setting. It is also partly due to genuine problems with the theory of parity sheaves in very small characteristic; a finer investigation of perverse parity sheaves may allow us to extend our results to all
\ell
. We hope to return to this in future work.

Finally, we recall that Treumann-Venkatesh pointed out
[
TV14
, §7.8]
that without the simply connected hypotheses, a
\sigma
-dual homomorphism need not exist with the “usual”
L
-group defined by Langlands, and they predicted that using instead the “
c
-group” might fix this issue. This prediction is morally consistent with our Theorem
7.6.1
since the
L
-group formed by taking the natural
W_{E}
-action on
\check{G}
is exactly this
c
-group: see
[
FS21
, VI.11]
and
[
Zhu17
, Remark 5.5.11]
.

7.6.1.
Compatibility with fiber functor

The fiber functor on
\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{G,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})
(resp.
\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{H,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})
) is given by relative cohomology over
S
,

\mathcal{F}\mapsto\bigoplus_{i}\mathrm{R}^{i}\pi_{G,S*}(\mathcal{F})\quad\text{(resp. $\mathcal{F}\mapsto\bigoplus_{i}\mathrm{R}^{i}\pi_{H,S*}(\mathcal{F})$)}

where

\pi_{G,S}\colon\Gr_{G,S/\Div_{X}^{1}}\rightarrow S\quad\text{resp.}\quad\pi_{H,S}\colon\Gr_{H,S/\Div_{X}^{1}}\rightarrow S

are the natural projections.

Proposition 7.6.4
.

The functor
br
is compatible with the fiber functors (cf. (
7.24
) below).

Proof.

Let
I=\operatorname{Gal}(\breve{E}^{s}/\breve{E})
be the inertia subgroup of
E
. For
c\in\pi_{1}(H)_{I}
, there is an open-closed embedding
\Gr_{H,S/\Div_{X}^{1}}(c)\hookrightarrow\Gr_{H,S/\Div_{X}^{1}}
. For a complex
\mathcal{K}\in\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{H,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{\mathcal{T}_{k}})
, this induces a decomposition
\mathcal{K}\cong\bigoplus_{c\in\pi_{1}(H)_{I}}\mathcal{K}(c)
. We write

\mathrm{T}^{\dagger_{H}^{G}}(\Gr_{H,S/\Div_{X}^{1}};\mathcal{K}):=\bigoplus_{c\in\pi_{1}(H)_{I}}\mathrm{T}^{\dagger_{H}^{G}(c)}(\Gr_{H,S/\Div_{X}^{1}};\mathcal{K}(c)),

i.e., we take Tate cohomology in degree
\dagger_{H}^{G}(c)
on the connected component
c
.

By Proposition
5.2.10
we have a natural isomorphism

(7.19)

\mathrm{T}^{0}(\mathrm{R}\pi_{G,S*}(\Nm\mathcal{F}))\cong\mathrm{T}^{0}(\mathrm{R}\pi_{H,S*}(\Psm\circ\Nm\mathcal{F})).

Then as in Remark
7.4.6
, we obtain a natural isomorphism

(7.20)

\mathrm{T}^{0}(\mathrm{R}\pi_{H,S*}(\Psm\circ\Nm\mathcal{F}))\cong\mathrm{T}^{\dagger_{H}^{G}}(\mathrm{R}\pi_{H,S*}(\mathbb{T}\circ br^{(\ell)}(\mathcal{F}))).

By Example
3.7.4
, we have

(7.21)

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}^{\dagger_{H}^{G}}(\mathrm{R}\pi_{H,S*}(\mathbb{T}\circ br^{(\ell)}(\mathcal{F})))

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbf{Z}}\mathrm{R}^{n}\pi_{H,S*}(br^{(\ell)}(\mathcal{F})).

Below we abbreviate the fiber functor on
\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{G,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})
as

\mathsf{F}^{G}:=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbf{Z}}\mathrm{R}^{n}\pi_{G,S*}\colon\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{G,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})\rightarrow\Loc(S;k)

and similarly for
H
. Putting together equations (
7.19
), (
7.20
), and (
7.21
), we have produced a natural isomorphism of
\Frob
-semilinear functors
\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{G,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})\rightarrow\Loc(S;k)
:

(7.22)

\mathrm{T}^{0}(\mathsf{F}^{G}(\Nm\mathcal{F}))\cong\mathsf{F}^{H}(br^{(\ell)}(\mathcal{F})).

By definition, we have

\mathsf{F}^{G}(\Nm(\mathcal{F}))\cong(\mathsf{F}^{G}(\mathcal{F}))\otimes\left({}^{\sigma}\mathsf{F}^{G}(\mathcal{F})\right)\otimes\ldots\otimes\left({}^{\sigma^{\ell-1}}\mathsf{F}^{G}(\mathcal{F})\right)\in\Loc(S;k)

with
\sigma
acting by cyclic rotation of the factors, so we have (cf. Example
3.7.2
) a natural isomorphism

(7.23)

\mathrm{T}^{0}(\mathsf{F}^{G}(\Nm(\mathcal{F})))\cong\mathsf{F}^{G}(\mathcal{F})^{(\ell)}.

Putting (
7.23
) into (
7.22
), we obtain the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{G,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{H,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Loc(S;k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Loc(S;k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces br^{(\ell)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{F}^{G})^{(\ell)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}^{H}}

Then applying linearization with respect to the
\mathbf{F}_{\ell}
-structure (
7.9
) gives the desired commutative diagram

(7.24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{G,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Parity_{\mathsf{n}}^{\ULA}(\Gr_{H,S/\Div^{1}_{X}};{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Loc(S;k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Loc(S;k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces br}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}^{H}}

∎

Corollary 7.6.5
.

The functor
br
is additive.

Proof.

The additivity can be checked after applying the fiber functor, since the latter is faithful. Then conclude using (
7.24
) and the additivity of the fiber functor
\mathsf{F}^{G}
.
∎

7.6.2.
Symmetric monoidality

We complete the proof of Theorem
7.4.5
with the Proposition below.

Proposition 7.6.6
.

The functor
br
promotes to a symmetric monoidal functor.

Proof.

First we promote
br
to a monoidal functor. For this it suffices to produce, naturally in
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, an isomorphism

(7.25)

\mathcal{B}r(\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(\mathcal{F}\star\mathcal{G}))\cong\mathcal{B}r(\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(\mathcal{F}))\star\mathcal{B}r(\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\mathcal{G}).

Since
\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}
has an evident symmetric monoidal structure, we rename
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}:=\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}:=\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\mathcal{G}
, and aim to produce a natural isomorphism

\mathcal{B}r(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\star\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{B}r(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\star\mathcal{B}r(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}).

Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}r(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\star\mathcal{G}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=L\circ[\dagger_{H}^{G}]\Psm(m_{!}(p_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\otimes p_{1}^{*}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\text{Lemma \ref{lem: small pullback} + Proposition \ref{prop: small equivariant localization}}\implies

\displaystyle\cong L\circ[\dagger_{H}^{G}]\circ m_{!}(p_{0}^{*}(\Psm\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\otimes p_{1}^{*}(\Psm\mathcal{G}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=L\circ[\dagger_{H}^{G}](\Psm\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\star\Psm\mathcal{G}^{\prime})

[\dagger_{H}^{G}]
symmetric monoidal
\implies

\displaystyle=L(([\dagger_{H}^{G}]\Psm\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\star([\dagger_{H}^{G}]\Psm\mathcal{G}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\text{Lemma \ref{lem: L monoidal}}\implies

\displaystyle\cong L([\dagger_{H}^{G}]\Psm\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\star L([\dagger_{H}^{G}]\Psm\mathcal{G}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}r(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\star\mathcal{B}r(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}).

This furnishes the monoidal structure (
7.25
).

Next we need to check that the monoidal functor
br
has the
property
of being symmetric monoidal. For this purpose, we may make a finite base change along
S
to reduce to the case where
G
is split, and has a Borus
(B,T)
. By Lemma
4.2.1
,
(B^{\sigma},T^{\sigma})=:(B_{H},T_{H})
is a Borus of
H
. Since
\CT_{B_{H}}
is faithful, it suffices to check the symmetric monoidality property after applying
\CT_{B_{H}}
. Using Lemma
7.5.2
, we are then reduced to the case where
G
and
H
are both tori. In this case, we have

\Gr_{T,S/\Div_{X}^{1}}=\coprod_{X_{*}(T)}S\quad\text{and}\quad\Gr_{T_{H},S/\Div_{X}^{1}}=\coprod_{X_{*}(T_{H})}S

and the commutativity constraints for
G,H
comes from convolution on
X_{*}(T),X_{*}(T_{H})
respectively. Then the symmetric monoidality is clear from inspection.
∎

The proof of Proposition
7.6.6
gives the following information about the induced map of tori.

Corollary 7.6.7
.

Let
\check{T}_{H},\check{T}
be the canonical maximal tori in
\check{H},\check{G}
, respectively. The restriction
\check{\psi}\colon\check{T}_{H}\rightarrow\check{T}
corresponds to the map
X^{*}(\check{T})\rightarrow X^{*}(\check{T}_{H})=X^{*}(\check{T})^{\sigma}
given by applying
N=(1+\sigma+\ldots+\sigma^{\ell-1})
.

7.7.
Multiple legs

For a finite non-empty set
I
, we abbreviate
\Sat_{G}^{I}(k):=\Sat(\Gr_{G,(\Div_{X}^{1})^{I}};k)
.

Theorem 7.7.1
.

There are Tannakian functors

\widetilde{br}^{I}\colon\Sat_{G}^{I}(k)\rightarrow\Sat_{H}^{I}(k)

for each non-empty finite set
I
, with the following properties.

(1) Under the Geometric Satake equivalence (
6.1
) of Fargues-Scholze,
\widetilde{br}^{I}
corresponds to the restriction
{}^{L}\psi^{*}\colon\Rep_{k}({}^{L}G)^{\otimes I}\rightarrow\Rep_{k}({}^{L}H)^{\otimes I}
induced by the
\sigma
-dual homomorphism
{}^{L}\psi\colon{}^{L}H\rightarrow{}^{L}G
from Corollary
7.6.2
. (In particular,
\widetilde{br}^{\{1\}}
agress with the
\widetilde{br}
from (
7.12
).)

(2) (Naturality in
I
) For any map of non-empty finite sets
\zeta\colon I\rightarrow J
, inducing the fusion product
\zeta\colon\Sat_{G}^{I}(k)\rightarrow\Sat_{G}^{J}(k)
and similarly for
H
, there is a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{G}^{I}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{H}^{I}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{G}^{J}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{H}^{J}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{br}^{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{br}^{J}}

such that the implicit natural isomorphisms are compatible with compositions
I\rightarrow J\rightarrow K
.

(3) For
\mathcal{F}\in\Sat_{G}^{I}(k)
, there are natural isomorphisms

\mathbb{T}\widetilde{br}^{I}(\mathcal{F})\cong{[\dagger_{H}^{G}]}\Psm(\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\mathcal{F})\in D^{\ULA}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{ck}_{H,(\Div_{X}^{1})^{I}};\mathcal{T}_{k})^{\bd}

compatible with any map of finite sets
\zeta\colon I\rightarrow J
as in (2).

Proof.

Write
I=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\{i\}
. We bootstrap from the case
|I|=1
using the convolution Hecke stack

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{ck}}_{G}^{I}:=\mathcal{H}\mathrm{ck}_{G}^{I;\{i\}_{i\in I}}\rightarrow(\Div^{1}_{X})^{I}

defined in
[
FS21
, p. 226]
. We have projection maps
p_{i}\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{ck}}_{G}^{I}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{ck}}_{G,\Div_{X}^{1}}^{\{i\}}
for each
i\in I
, as well as a convolution map
m\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathrm{ck}}_{G}^{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}\mathrm{ck}_{G,(\Div_{X}^{1})^{I}}
. The map

(\mathcal{K}_{i})_{i\in I}\mapsto Rm_{!}(\otimes_{i\in I}\ p_{i}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{i})\in\Sat_{G}^{I}(k)

induces an equivalence

\conv\colon\Sat(\Gr_{G,\Div^{1}_{X}})^{\otimes I}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sat_{G}^{I}(k).

The same considerations apply to
H
. We define
\widetilde{br}^{I}\colon\Sat_{G}^{I}(k)\rightarrow\Sat_{H}^{I}(k)
by the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{G}(k)^{\otimes I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{H}(k)^{\otimes I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{G}^{I}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{H}^{I}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\conv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{br}^{\otimes I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\conv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{br}^{I}}

Then property (1) follows from the
|I|=1
case, which was arranged in Corollary
7.6.2
, and Tannakian reconstruction
[
FS21
, §11]
.

For property (2), recall that the fusion product
\Sat_{G}^{I}(k)\rightarrow\Sat_{G}^{J}(k)
is arranged in
[
FS21
, §VI.9.4]
so that under the identifications in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat(\Gr_{G,\Div^{1}_{X}})^{\otimes I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat(\Gr_{G,\Div^{1}_{X}})^{\otimes J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{G}^{I}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{G}^{J}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\conv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\conv}

it corresponds to

\star_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i}\mapsto\otimes_{j\in J}\left(\star_{i\in\zeta^{-1}(j)}\mathcal{F}_{j}\right)

in the top row. Hence the compatibility of
\widetilde{br}^{I}
in (2) is equivalent to the symmetric monoidality of
\widetilde{br}
with respect to the fusion product, which was established in Theorem
7.6.1
.

Property (3) is arranged by construction for
I=\{1\}
: see (
7.13
). For
|I|>1
, we consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{G}^{\{1\}}(k)^{\otimes I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\ULA}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{ck}_{H,(\Div_{X}^{1})^{I}};\mathcal{T}_{k})^{\otimes I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{H}^{\{1\}}(k)^{\otimes I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{G}^{I}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\ULA}(\mathcal{H}\mathrm{ck}_{H,(\Div^{1}_{X})^{I}};\mathcal{T}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sat_{H}^{I}(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\conv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({[\dagger_{H}^{G}]}\circ\Psm\circ\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})})^{\otimes I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{br}^{\otimes I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\conv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\conv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}^{\otimes I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{br}^{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\dagger_{H}^{G}]}\circ\Psm\circ\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}

The outermost rectangle commutes by definition of
\widetilde{br}^{I}
. The top cap is the
I
th tensor power of the case
|I|=1
, so it commutes. The right square commutes by compatibility of
\mathbb{T}
with pushforward and pullback. The left square commutes by (symmetric) monoidality of
\Nm
, compatibility of
\Psm
with pushforward and pullback (§
5.2.3
), and monoidality of
[\dagger_{H}^{G}]
. Hence the bottom cap commutes, which gives (3) for general
I
.

∎

8.
Tate cohomology of moduli of local shtukas

In this section we will integrate the Brauer functor into the construction of the Fargues-Scholze correspondence (
1.3
) for
H
and for
G
, in order to deduce information about functoriality.

In §
8.1
– §
8.4
we review a construction of the Fargues-Scholze correspondence, which resembles a local version of the work of Vincent Lafforgue
[
Laf18
]
in the global setting, but (amazingly!) applies equally well in mixed characteristic. Our starting point is the
moduli space of local shtukas

\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
, defined from the input data of a reductive group
G/E
, an element
b
in the “Kottwitz set”
B(G)
, a finite non-empty set
I
, and a compact open subgroup
K\subset G(E)
. A generalization of the Grothendieck-Messing period map gives an étale morphism from
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
to a “twisted” version of the Beilinson-Drinfeld affine Grassmannian
\Gr^{\twi}_{G,\prod_{i\in I}\Spd\breve{E}}
. By pulling back sheaves along this morphism, the Geometric Satake equivalence of Fargues-Scholze supplies a functor from
\Rep_{k}(({}^{L}G)^{I})
to sheaves on
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
, which are “compatible with fusion” in a suitable sense. According to V. Lafforgue’s paradigm, such a collection of functors gives rise to commuting
excursion operators
on the cohomology of
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
, whose simultaneous generalized eigenvalues correspond naturally to semisimple Galois representations.

To study functoriality, we link the processes outlined in the preceding paragraph for
G
and for
H
using the Brauer functor from §
7
. (The reader may find it helpful to consult Figure
2
again.) Thanks to the calculations in §
4
we may realize
\Sht_{(H,b^{\prime},I),\iota^{*}K}
as (an open-closed subset of) the
\Sigma
-fixed points of
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
, and the Brauer functor
\widetilde{br}^{I}
gives a geometric link between the sheaves on
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
indexed by
V\in\Rep_{k}(({}^{L}G)^{I})
and the sheaves on
\Sht_{(H,b^{\prime},I),\iota^{*}K}
indexed by
{}^{L}\psi^{*}(V)\in\Rep_{k}(({}^{L}H)^{I})
. We feed this link into equivariant localization for Tate cohomology, which is part of the formalism developed in §
3
. In §
8.5
we define and study excursion operators on Tate cohomology of the moduli spaces of local shtukas. Then in §
8.6
, we extract functoriality relations between excursion operators for
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
and for
\Sht_{(H,b^{\prime},I),\iota^{*}K}
.

8.1.
Moduli spaces of local shtukas

The definitions of local shtukas in
p
-adic geometry are developed in Scholze’s Berkeley Lectures on
p
-adic geometry, especially
[
SW20
, Lecture XXIII]
. Properties of the cohomology of their moduli spaces are established in
[
FS21
, §IX.3]
. We recall some relevant aspects here.

8.1.1.
Setup

Recall that
G
is a reductive group over
E
. For each finite set
I
,
b\in B(G)
, and compact open subgroup
K<G(E)
, we have a moduli space of local shtukas
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
. By
[
SW20
, Theorem 23.1.4]
, it is an inductive limit of locally spatial diamonds with finite cohomological dimension along a countable index set. Furthermore, there is an action of
G_{b}(E)
on
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
.

The space
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
comes equipped with “leg” maps

f_{K}\colon\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}\Spd\breve{E}

which are partially proper and ind-shriekable, and Grothendieck-Messing period maps

\pi_{K}\colon\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}\rightarrow\Gr^{\twi}_{G,\prod_{i\in I}\Spd\breve{E}}.

which are étale.

8.1.2.
Satake sheaves

As in
[
FS21
, §IX]
, we choose a square root of the cyclotomic character
W_{E}\rightarrow k^{\times}
in order to trivialize the cyclotomic twist in the Geometric Satake equivalence, giving a
W_{E}
-equivariant isomorphism
\check{G}\cong\widehat{G}
.

Let
Q
be a finite quotient of
W_{E}
over which the action on
\widehat{G}
-factors. For any finite set
I
and
W\in\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})
, the Geometric Satake equivalence gives a relative perverse sheaf
\mathcal{S}_{W}
on
\Gr^{\twi}_{G,\prod_{i\in I}\Spd\breve{E}}
(cf.
[
FS21
, §I.9, §IX.3]
) which we pull back via
\pi_{K}^{*}
to
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
, and we also denote the resulting complex by
\mathcal{S}_{W}\in D_{\et}^{b}(\Sht_{(G,b,I),K};k)
.

8.1.3.
Cohomology of moduli spaces of local shtukas

By
[
FS21
, Corollary I.7.3, Proposition IX.3.2]
, we may regard

(8.1)

\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{W}\in D(\Rep^{\mathrm{sm}}_{k}G_{b}(E))^{B\prod_{i\in I}W_{E}}

as a (derived) representation of
G_{b}(E)
with a commuting action of
\prod_{i\in I}W_{E}
. To demystify this a bit: the
G_{b}(E)
-action on cohomology is induced by the
G_{b}(E)
-action on the space
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
, and the
\prod_{i\in I}W_{E}
-action comes from a natural descent (using an interpretation via Hecke operators) of
\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{W}
from
\prod_{i\in I}\Spd\breve{E}
to
\prod_{i\in I}[\Spd\breve{E}/\varphi]\cong\prod_{i\in I}[\Spd C/W_{E}]
.

Example 8.1.1
.

For
W=\mathbbm{1}
the trivial representation,
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
is only non-empty for
b=1_{G}
. In this case,
\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbbm{1}}
is
\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}(k)
with the obvious
G(E)
-action and the trivial
W_{E}
-action.

The functor

\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})\rightarrow D(\Rep^{\mathrm{sm}}_{k}G_{b}(E))^{B\prod_{i\in I}W_{E}}

sending
\mathcal{S}_{W}\mapsto\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{W}
satisfies the following
fusion compatibility
. Any map of finite non-empty sets
\zeta\colon I\rightarrow J
induces a map
({}^{L}G)^{J}\rightarrow({}^{L}G)^{I}
. Let
\Res_{\zeta}\colon\Rep_{k}(({}^{L}G)^{I})\rightarrow\Rep_{k}(({}^{L}G)^{J})
be the restriction functor along this map. By construction, Geometric Satake is arranged so that the corresponding functor
\Sat_{G}^{I}(k)\rightarrow\Sat_{G}^{J}(k)
is the fusion product. We have commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sht_{(G,b,J),K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gr^{\twi}_{G,\prod_{j\in J}\Spd\breve{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gr^{\twi}_{G,\prod_{i\in I}\Spd\breve{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{j\in J}\Spd\breve{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i\in I}\Spd\breve{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{K}}

The Geometric Satake equivalence is constructed in
[
FS21
, §VI]
so that one has
\mathcal{S}_{W}|_{\Gr^{\twi}_{G,\prod_{j\in J}\Spd\breve{E}}}\cong\mathcal{S}_{\Res_{\zeta}W}
, naturally in
W\in\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})
and compatibly under compositions of finite sets. This induces a natural isomorphism

(8.2)

\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{W}\cong\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{\Res_{\zeta}(W)}

of functors
\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})\rightarrow D(\Rep^{\mathrm{sm}}_{k}G_{b}(E))^{B\prod_{j\in J}W_{E}}
. Moreover, the natural isomorphism (
8.2
) under compositions of maps of finite sets.

8.2.
Excursion algebra

An
excursion datum
for
\widehat{G}
(over
k
)
[
FS21
, Definition VIII.4.2]
is a tuple
\mathcal{D}=(I,V,\alpha,\beta,(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})
where:

•

I
is a finite set and
\gamma_{i}\in W_{E}
for each
i\in I
,

•

V\in\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})
for varying
Q
, and
\mathbbm{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha}V|_{\widehat{G}}
and
V|_{\widehat{G}}\xrightarrow{\beta}\mathbbm{1}
are maps of
\widehat{G}
-representations. (Here
\mathbbm{1}
is the trivial representation.)

The
excursion algebra

\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
is the
k
-algebra on generator
S_{\mathcal{D}}
for each excursion datum
\mathcal{D}
, with relations as in
[
Fen24
, §2.4]
. Another definition appears in
[
FS21
, Definition VIII.3.4]
.

An
L
-parameter
(with coefficients in
k
) is a class in
\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{G}(k))
, or equivalently a section
W_{E}\rightarrow\widehat{G}(k)\rtimes W_{E}
up to
\widehat{G}(k)
-conjugation. An
L
-parameter is
semisimple
if whenever it factors through a parabolic
{}^{L}P(k)\subset{}^{L}G(k)
, it also factors through a Levi
{}^{L}M(k)\subset{}^{L}P(k)

[
FS21
, Definition VIII.3.1]
.

Combining
[
FS21
, Proposition VIII.3.8]
and the statement above
[
FS21
, Definition VIII.3.4]
that the natural map from
\Exc(W_{E},\widehat{G})
to the
spectral Bernstein center

\mathcal{O}(Z^{1}(W_{E},\widehat{G}))^{\widehat{G}}
is a universal homeomorphism, we obtain a canonical bijection between

\{\text{characters $\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\rightarrow k$}\}\longleftrightarrow\{\text{semisimple $L$-parameters $\rho\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{G}(k))$}\}.

Suppose we are given a homomorphism
{}^{L}\psi\colon{}^{L}H\rightarrow{}^{L}G
. Then for any excursion datum
\mathcal{D}=(I,V,\alpha,\beta,(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})
for
\widehat{G}
, we define

{}^{L}\psi^{*}(\mathcal{D}):=(I,{}^{L}\psi^{*}(V),{}^{L}\psi^{*}(\alpha),{}^{L}\psi^{*}(\beta),(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})

as an excursion datum for
\widehat{H}
. The map
S_{\mathcal{D}}\mapsto S_{{}^{L}\psi^{*}(\mathcal{D})}
defines a homomorphism

(8.3)

{}^{L}\psi^{*}\colon\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\rightarrow\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H}).

On spectra, it sends (the point corresponding to) a semisimple
L
-parameter
\rho\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{H}(k))
to (the point corresponding to) the semisimple
L
-parameter
{}^{L}\psi\circ\rho\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{G}(k))
.

8.3.
The Bernstein center

Recall that in (
7.1
) we defined the Hecke algebra
\mathscr{H}(G,K;\Lambda)
for a compact open subgroup
K\subset G(E)
. We let
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K;\Lambda):=Z(\mathscr{H}(G,K;\Lambda))
be the center of
\mathscr{H}(G,K;\Lambda)
.

If
K\subset K^{\prime}
have prime-to-
\ell
pro-order (e.g., this will be true as long as they are sufficiently small), then convolution with
\mathbbm{1}_{K^{\prime}}
gives a homomorphism
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K;\Lambda)\rightarrow\mathfrak{Z}(G,K^{\prime};\Lambda)
. The
Bernstein center of
G
(with coefficients in
\Lambda
)
is

\mathfrak{Z}(G;\Lambda):=\varprojlim_{K}\mathfrak{Z}(G,K;\Lambda),

where the transition maps are as above, and the inverse limit is taken over
K
with prime-to-
\ell
pro-order.

If
\Lambda=k
, we abbreviate
\mathscr{H}(G,K):=\mathscr{H}(G,K;k)
,
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K):=\mathfrak{Z}(G,K;k)
, and
\mathfrak{Z}(G):=\mathfrak{Z}(G;k)
.

The Bernstein center
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
may also be identified with the ring of endomorphisms of the identity functor of the category
\Rep_{k}^{\mathrm{sm}}(G(E))
. In particular, any irreducible admissible representation
\Pi
of
G(E)
over
k
induces a character of
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
.

8.4.
Review of Fargues-Scholze correspondence

Fargues-Scholze construct in
[
FS21
]
a
k
-algebra homomorphism

(8.4)

\FS_{G}\colon\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\rightarrow\mathfrak{Z}(G;k).

Via (
8.4
),
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
acts through a character on any irreducible admissible representation
\Pi\in\Rep_{k}^{\mathrm{sm}}(G(E))
, and the corresponding semisimple
L
-parameter is denoted
\rho_{\Pi}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{G}(k))
.

We present the construction of (
8.4
) on
[
FS21
, p.36]
. Fix
b:=1_{G}\in B(G)
. Let

\overline{x}\colon\Spd\widehat{\overline{E}}\rightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Spd\breve{E}

be the geometric diagonal, and
f_{K}^{\Delta}\colon\Sht_{(G,1_{G},I),K}^{\Delta}\rightarrow\Spd\widehat{\overline{E}}
be the base change of
f_{K}
along
\overline{x}
.

Let
K\subset G(E)
be a compact open subgroup. Recall from Example
8.1.1
that the underlying
G(E)
-representation of
\mathrm{R}f_{K!}^{\Delta}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbbm{1}}
is
\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k
. Hence for each excursion datum
\mathcal{D}=(I,V,\alpha,\beta,(\gamma_{i})_{i\in i})
we have a composition

(8.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}f_{K!}^{\Delta}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbbm{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}f_{K!}^{\Delta}\mathcal{S}_{V|_{\widehat{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{V})_{\overline{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{V})_{\overline{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}f_{K!}^{\Delta}\mathcal{S}_{V|_{\widehat{G}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}f_{K!}^{\Delta}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbbm{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FS_{G}(S_{\mathcal{D}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

(in the middle step we used (
8.1
) to obtain an action of
\prod_{i\in I}W_{E}
on
(\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{V})_{\overline{x}}
) which defines an element
\FS_{G}(S_{\mathcal{D}})\in\End_{G(E)}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k,\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)\cong\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)
. As
K
varies, these elements are compatible under convolution, hence assemble to an element of
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
. The map (
8.4
) sends
S_{\mathcal{D}}
to this element of
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
.

8.5.
Excursion operators on Tate cohomology

Recall the notion of
Tate cohomology
from §
3.7
. We will now study excursion operators on the Tate cohomology of
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
.

8.5.1.
Tate cohomology of moduli of shtukas

Recall that
G
has a given action of
\Sigma
. Assume that the level structure
K\subset G(E)
is
\Sigma
-invariant, and
b\in B(G)
is
\Sigma
-fixed. Then there is an induced
\Sigma
-action on
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
.

The given action of
\Sigma
on
G
induces an action
V\mapsto{}^{\sigma}V
of
\Sigma
on
\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})
. Suppose we have a
\Sigma
-equivariant representation
W\in\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})^{B\Sigma}
. Then
\mathcal{S}_{W}
has the structure of a
\Sigma
-equivariant sheaf on
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
. This equips its cohomology with a
\Sigma
-equivariant structure, so that we may regard, using (
8.1
),

\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{W}\in D(\Rep^{\mathrm{sm}}_{k}G_{b}(E))^{B(\prod_{i\in I}W_{E}\rtimes\Sigma)}.

Hence we can form the
j
th Tate cohomology of
\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{W}
(for
j\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
), which we denote

(8.6)

\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Sht_{(G,b,I),K};W):=\mathrm{T}^{j}(\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{W})\in D(\Rep^{\mathrm{sm}}_{k}G_{b}^{\sigma}(E))^{B\prod_{i\in I}W_{E}}.

as we will want to record the dependence on
G,b,I
.

Example 8.5.1
.

For
V=\mathbbm{1}
, the trivial representation,
\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}
is only non-empty for
b=1_{G}
. In that case, we have (cf. Example
8.1.1
)

\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Sht_{(G,1_{G},I),K};\mathbbm{1})\cong\mathrm{T}^{j}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)\in\Rep^{\mathrm{sm}}_{k}(H(E)).

A similar story applies to
H
. Note that
\Sigma
acts trivially on
\Sht_{(H,b,I),K}
, so if
\Sigma
also acts trivially on
W\in\Rep_{k}((\widehat{H}\rtimes Q)^{I})
, then by Example
3.7.4
and Example
3.7.1
, we have for each
j\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
a natural isomorphism

(8.7)

\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Sht_{(H,b,I),K};W)\cong\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbf{Z}}\mathrm{H}^{n}(\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{W})=\mathrm{H}^{*}(\mathrm{R}f_{K!}\mathcal{S}_{W}).

8.5.2.
Excursion action

The
\Sigma
action on
G
induces a
\Sigma
-action on
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
by transport of structure. Concretely,
\Sigma
acts on excursion data by

\sigma\cdot(I,V,\alpha,\beta,(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})=(I,{}^{\sigma}V,\sigma(\alpha),\sigma(\beta),(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})

and then
\sigma\cdot S_{\mathcal{D}}=S_{\sigma\cdot\mathcal{D}}\in\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
.

In general, given a
k[\Sigma]
-algebra
A
and an
A
-module
M
, there is a natural
\mathrm{T}^{0}(A)=A^{\sigma}/(N\cdot A)
-module structure on
\mathrm{T}^{*}(M)
. In particular, this equips
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Sht_{(G,b,I),K};W)
with a natural
\mathrm{T}^{0}\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
-action. We are most interested in the special case where
W=\mathbbm{1}
and
b=1_{G}
, in which case we have
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Sht_{(G,b,I),K};\mathbbm{1})=\mathrm{T}^{j}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)
at the level of underlying
H(E)
-representations (cf. Example
8.5.1
). If an excursion datum
\mathcal{D}=(I,V,\alpha,\beta,(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})
is
\Sigma
-invariant, then
S_{\mathcal{D}}\in\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}
and its action on
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)
can be described more concretely using (
8.5
): it is given by composition

(8.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{j}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{j}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Sht_{(G,1,I),K};\mathbbm{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Sht_{(G,1,I),K};V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Sht_{(G,1,I),K};V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Sht_{(G,1,I),K};\mathbbm{1})}
(
8.5.1
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

8.5.3.
Normed excursion action

Recall from Definition
7.1.1
that for any commutative
k[\Sigma]
-algebra
A
, there is the
Tate diagonal
morphism

A\xrightarrow{\Nm}\mathrm{T}^{0}(A)

sending
a
to the class of
\Nm(a)=a\cdot\sigma(a)\cdots\sigma^{\ell-1}(a)
. This is
\Frob
-semilinear, but an
\mathbf{F}_{\ell}[\Sigma]
-structure on
A
induces a linearization
\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\colon A\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{0}(A)
, which is a
k
-algebra homomorphism.

We apply this to
A:=\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
, with the
\mathbf{F}_{\ell}
-structure coming from the fact that
\widehat{G}
is defined over
\mathbf{F}_{\ell}
. In §
8.5.2
we saw an action of
\mathrm{T}^{0}\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
on
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)
. Inflating this action through
\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}
gives an action of
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
on
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)
, which we call the
normed excursion action
.

8.5.4.
Native excursion action

The
\Sigma
-invariant subalgebra
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}
acts naturally on
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Sht_{(G,b,I),K};V)
via the quotient
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G}))
and then the mechanism of §
8.5.2
. We refer to this as the
native excursion action
of
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}
on
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Sht_{(G,b,I),K};V)
. This action is
not
the same as the restriction of the normed excursion action to the subalgebra
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}\subset\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
; this discrepancy will be responsible for Frobenius twists which show up later.

In general, for a commutative
k
-algebra
A
the absolute Frobenius
A\rightarrow A
is
\Frob
-semilinear. If
A
has an
\mathbf{F}_{\ell}
-structure
A\cong A_{0}\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{\ell}}k
, then the absolute Frobenius may be linearized to a map
F\colon A\rightarrow A
, as explained in §
7.1.2
. The map
F
is characterized as the unique
k
-linear homomorphism that sends
a_{0}\mapsto a_{0}^{\ell}
for all
a_{0}\in A_{0}\subset A
.

The preceding discussion applies to
A:=\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}
(with the
\mathbf{F}_{\ell}
-structure coming from the fact that
\widehat{G}
is defined over
\mathbf{F}_{\ell}
). In these terms, the normed excursion action of
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}
is the native excursion action composed with the map
F\colon\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}\rightarrow\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}
which is the linearization of the absolute Frobenius.

8.5.5.
Norm of excursion data

For any finite group
Q
over which the
W_{E}
-action on
\widehat{G}
factors, we define a functor

\Nm\colon\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})\rightarrow\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})^{B\Sigma}

as follows:

•

For a representation
V\in\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})
, we set

\Nm(V):=V\otimes_{k}\left({}^{\sigma}V\right)\otimes_{k}\ldots\otimes_{k}\left({}^{\sigma^{\ell-1}}V\right)\in\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})^{B\Sigma}

with the obvious
\Sigma
-equivariant structure. Note that it corresponds under Geometric Satake to Definition
7.2.5
.

•

Given
h\colon V\rightarrow V^{\prime}\in\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})
, we set

\Nm(h):=h\otimes{}^{\sigma}h\otimes\ldots\otimes{}^{\sigma^{\ell-1}}h\colon\Nm(V)\rightarrow\Nm(V^{\prime}).

Note that
\Nm
is
not
an additive functor, nor is it even
k
-linear.

Definition 8.5.2
.

We define the
linearized norm

\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}:=\Nm\circ\Frob^{-1}
to be the linearization of
\Nm
in the sense of Construction
7.4.1
; note that
\Frob^{-1}
is the identity on objects and on morphisms it is
(-)\otimes_{k,\Frob^{-1}}k
. Then the resulting functor

\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\colon\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})\rightarrow\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})^{B\Sigma}

is
k
-linear (although still not additive).

Definition 8.5.3
.

For
V\in\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})
, we denote by

N\cdot V\in\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})^{B\Sigma}

the
\sigma
-equivariant representation
V\oplus{}^{\sigma}V\oplus\ldots\oplus{}^{\sigma^{\ell-1}}V
, with the obvious
\Sigma
-equivariant structure.

For
h\colon V\rightarrow V^{\prime}\in\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})
, we denote by

N\cdot h\colon N\cdot V\rightarrow N\cdot V^{\prime}

the
\sigma
-equivariant map
h\oplus{}^{\sigma}h\oplus\ldots\oplus{}^{\sigma^{\ell-1}}h
. Let
\Delta_{\ell}\colon\mathbbm{1}\rightarrow\mathbbm{1}^{\oplus\ell}
denote the diagonal map and
\nabla_{\ell}\colon\mathbbm{1}^{\oplus\ell}\rightarrow\mathbbm{1}
denote the sum map.

Definition 8.5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=(I,V,\alpha,\beta,(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})
be an excursion datum for
\widehat{G}
. Define the excursion data

\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\mathcal{D}:=(I,\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}V,\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\alpha,\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\beta,(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})

and

N\cdot\mathcal{D}:=(I,N\cdot V,(N\cdot\alpha)\circ\Delta_{\ell},\nabla_{\ell}\circ(N\cdot\beta),(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I}),

which are excursion data for
\widehat{G}
.

Straightforward calculations yield:

Lemma 8.5.5

(
[
Fen24
, Lemma 5.16]
)
.

For all excursion data
\mathcal{D}
, we have

\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(S_{\mathcal{D}})=S_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(\mathcal{D})}\in\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}

and

N\cdot S_{\mathcal{D}}=S_{N\cdot\mathcal{D}}\in\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}.

Proof.

The second identity appears in
[
Fen24
, Lemma 5.16]
, which also proves that

\Nm(S_{\mathcal{D}})=S_{\Nm(\mathcal{D})}\in\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}

from which the first identity follows.
∎

8.6.
Functoriality for excursion operators

For basic
b\in B(H)
, we let
H_{b}
be the corresponding inner twist of
H
, as in §
4.4
. If
b\in B(H)
is basic and maps to
1_{G}\in B(G)
, then we have
\iota_{b^{\prime}}\colon H_{b^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow G_{\iota(b^{\prime})}=G
. Below we abbreviate
\iota^{*}K:=\iota_{b^{\prime}}^{*}K
and
1
for the trivial element of
B(G)
or
B(H_{b^{\prime}})
, depending on context.

Proposition 8.6.1
.

Assume
\ell>\max\{b(\widehat{G}),b(\widehat{H})\}
. Let
K<G(E)
be an open subgroup stable under
\Sigma
, and with prime-to-
\ell
pro-order. Then for any
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
and each non-empty finite set
I
, there is a natural isomorphism

(8.9)

\mathrm{T}^{\varepsilon}(\Sht_{(G,1,I),K};\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(V))\cong\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{T}^{\varepsilon}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};{}^{L}\psi^{*}(V))

of functors

\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})\rightarrow D(\Rep^{\mathrm{sm}}_{k}H(E))^{B\prod_{i\in I}W_{E}}.

Moreover, these natural isomorphisms are compatible with fusion along all maps of non-empty finite sets
\zeta\colon I\rightarrow J
.

The meaning of the last sentence is as follows. As explained in §
8.1.3
, any such
\zeta\colon I\rightarrow J
induces a restriction functor
\Res_{\zeta}\colon\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{I})\rightarrow\Rep_{k}((\widehat{G}\rtimes Q)^{J})
. The natural isomorphism (
8.2
) induces a natural isomorphism

(8.10)

\mathrm{T}^{\varepsilon}(\Sht_{(G,1,I),K};W)\cong\mathrm{T}^{\varepsilon}(\Sht_{(G,1,J),K};\Res_{\zeta}W)

compatible with compositions of maps of finite sets, and similarly for each
b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})
a natural isomorphism

(8.11)

\mathrm{T}^{\varepsilon}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};{}^{L}\psi^{*}(W))\cong\mathrm{T}^{\varepsilon}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},J),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};{}^{L}\psi^{*}(\Res_{\zeta}W))

compatible with compositions of maps of finite sets. Then “compatible with fusion” means that for every
\zeta\colon I\rightarrow J
, the diagram below commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\varepsilon}(\Sht_{(G,1,I),K};\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{T}^{\varepsilon}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};{}^{L}\psi^{*}(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{\varepsilon}(\Sht_{(G,1,J),K};\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(\Res_{\zeta}V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{T}^{\varepsilon}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},J),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};{}^{L}\psi^{*}(\Res_{\zeta}V))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
(
8.10
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
(
8.9
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
(
8.11
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
(
8.9
)

Proof.

For each
b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})\subset B(H)
, the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gr^{\twi}_{H,\prod_{i\in I}\Spd\breve{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sht_{(G,b,I),K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gr^{\twi}_{G,\prod_{i\in I}\Spd\breve{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\iota^{*}K}^{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{K}^{G}}

induces a natural isomorphism
\iota^{*}(\pi_{K}^{G})^{*}\cong(\pi_{\iota^{*}K}^{H})^{*}\iota^{*}
, hence natural isomorphisms

(8.12)

\mathbb{T}^{*}\iota^{*}(\pi_{K}^{G})^{*}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(V)}\right)\cong\mathbb{T}^{*}(\pi_{\iota^{*}K}^{H})^{*}\iota^{*}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(V)}\right)\cong(\pi_{\iota^{*}K}^{H})^{*}\mathbb{T}^{*}\iota^{*}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(V)}\right)\in\Shv(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};\mathcal{T}_{k}),

compatible with fusion. Recalling that
\Psm=\mathbb{T}^{*}\iota^{*}
, Theorem
7.7.1
supplies natural isomorphisms

(8.13)

(\pi_{\iota^{*}K}^{H})^{*}\mathbb{T}^{*}\iota^{*}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(V)}\right)\cong(\pi_{\iota^{*}K}^{H})^{*}\mathbb{T}^{*}\left(\mathcal{S}_{{}^{L}\psi^{*}(V)}\right)\in\Shv(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};\mathcal{T}_{k}),

compatible with fusion.

We now consider Tate cohomology of the moduli of shtukas. To distinguish between
G
and
H
, we write

\displaystyle f_{K}^{G}

\displaystyle\colon\Sht_{(G,1,I),K}\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}\Spd\breve{E},

\displaystyle f_{K}^{H,b^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\colon\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}}\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}\Spd\breve{E}.

Note that support of any Satake sheaf on
\Sht_{(G,1,I),K}
has finite
\mathrm{dim.}\,\mathrm{trg}\,
over
\prod\Spd\breve{E}
and is extra small (cf. Example
3.1.4
), and similarly for
\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}}
. As
\Fix(\sigma,\Sht_{(G,b,I),K})
is a finite union of spaces of the form
\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}}
by Proposition
4.4.7
, the results of §
3
apply. In particular, we may apply Proposition
3.6.2
, which relates the Tate cohomology of
\Sht_{(G,1,I),K}
to that of its fixed points, and Proposition
4.4.7
, which identifies these fixed points; combining these with (
8.12
) and (
8.13
) gives a sequence of natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathrm{R}(f_{K}^{G})_{!}((\pi_{K}^{G})^{*}\mathcal{S}_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(V)})

\displaystyle\text{Propositions \ref{prop: equivariant localization} \& \ref{prop: fixed points of sht}}\implies

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{R}(f_{K}^{H,b^{\prime}})_{!}\mathbb{T}^{*}\iota^{*}((\pi_{K}^{G})^{*}\mathcal{S}_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(V)})

\displaystyle\eqref{eq: eqloc 1}\implies

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{R}(f_{K}^{H,b^{\prime}})_{!}(\pi_{\iota^{*}K}^{H})^{*}\mathbb{T}^{*}\iota^{*}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(V)}\right)

\displaystyle\eqref{eq: eqloc 2}\implies

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{R}(f_{K}^{H,b^{\prime}})_{!}(\pi_{\iota^{*}K}^{H})^{*}\mathbb{T}^{*}\left(\mathcal{S}_{{}^{L}\psi^{*}(V)}\right),

compatible with fusion. The result then follows upon applying the Tate cohomology functor
\mathrm{T}^{\varepsilon}(-)
.
∎

In particular, when
V=\mathbbm{1}
is the trivial representation we have an identification

(8.14)

\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(G,1,\{0\}),K};\mathbbm{1})\cong\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},\{0\}),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};\mathbbm{1})

Note that the
H_{b^{\prime}}
for
b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})
are inner twists of each other, so their
L
-groups
{}^{L}H_{b^{\prime}}
are all canonically identified, hence we may identify each of their excursion algebras with
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})
.

Theorem 8.6.2
.

Assume
\ell>\max\{b(\widehat{G}),b(\widehat{H})\}
. Let
K<G(E)
be a prime-to-
\ell
compact open subgroup stable under
\Sigma
. Then the isomorphism (
8.14
) is equivariant for the action of
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
, acting on the LHS via the normed excursion action (§
8.5.3
) and on the RHS through the homomorphism
{}^{L}\psi^{*}\colon\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\rightarrow\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})
from (
8.3
) followed by the native excursion action (§
8.5.4
).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{D}=(I,V,\alpha,\beta,(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})
be an excursion datum for
\widehat{G}
. By the definition of the excursion action (cf. (
8.5
)), what we have to show is that the action of
\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}S_{\mathcal{D}}\in\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}
on
\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(G,1,\{0\}),K};\mathbbm{1})
is intertwined with the action of
S_{{}^{L}\psi^{*}(\mathcal{D})}\in\Exc(W_{E},{}^{L}H)
on
\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},\{0\}),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};\mathbbm{1})
under the identification (
8.14
).

By Lemma
8.5.5
we have
\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}S_{\mathcal{D}}=S_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\mathcal{D}}
, whose excursion action is the composition in the left column in the diagram below:

(8.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(G,1,I),K};\mathbbm{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};\mathbbm{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(G,1,I),K};\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};{}^{L}\psi^{*}(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(G,1,I),K};\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};{}^{L}\psi^{*}(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(G,1,I),K};\mathbbm{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};\mathbbm{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\psi^{*}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\psi^{*}(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Meanwhile,
S_{{}^{L}\psi^{*}(\mathcal{D})}
is the composition in the right column in the diagram. Proposition
8.6.1
establishes the horizontal identifications making all diagrams commutes, which gives the result.
∎

9.
Derived Treumann-Venkatesh Conjecture

In this section we will reap the applications of the preceding material, especially the functoriality relations for excursion operators from Theorem
8.6.2
. In §
9.1
we formulate the “derived” version of Treumann-Venkatesh’s Conjecture
1.2.3
(2), and prove it in §
9.4
. In §
9.2
we will construct the Treumann-Venkatesh homomorphism alluded to in (
1.9
), and in §
9.3
we establish the commutative square (
1.10
). Finally, in §
9.5
we prove the existence of functorial lifts along
\sigma
-dual homomorphisms, including Theorem
1.4.1
.

9.1.
Formulation

By the realization of the Bernstein center
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
as the ring of endomorphisms of the identity functor on
D^{b}(\Rep_{k}^{\sm}G(E))
, there is a tautological action of
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
on any
\Pi\in D^{b}(\Rep_{k}^{\sm}G(E))
. Composing this with the Fargues-Scholze map
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\xrightarrow{\FS_{G}}\mathfrak{Z}(G;k)
discussed in §
8.4
, we obtain an action of
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
on any such
\Pi
. In particular, to each
\Pi
we may attach a subset

\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}(\mathrm{H}^{*}(\Pi))\subset\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G}),

which can be interpreted as the “set of semi-simple
L
-parameters attached to
\Pi
”. For example, if
\Pi\in\Rep_{k}^{\sm}G(E)
is irreducible admissible, then
\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}(\Pi)
is necessarily a single closed point, corresponding to the
Fargues-Scholze parameter

\rho_{\Pi}\in H^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{G}(k))
. This defines the map (
1.3
).

Let
\Pi\in D^{b}(\Rep_{k}^{\sm}(G(E)\rtimes\Sigma))=D^{b}(\Rep_{k}^{\sm}G(E))^{B\Sigma}
. Then we may form the Tate cohomology
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi)
, which is naturally an object of
\Rep_{k}^{\sm}H(E)
.

Theorem 9.1.1
.

Let
F\colon\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\rightarrow\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
be the linearized Frobenius (cf. §
8.5.4
), which is a
k
-algebra homomorphism. This induces a functor
F_{*}
on
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
-modules.

Assume
\ell>\max\{b(\widehat{G}),b(\widehat{H})\}
. Then for any
j\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
,
\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})}(\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi))
lies over
F_{*}\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}(\mathrm{H}^{*}(\Pi))
with respect to the map
\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})\xrightarrow{{}^{L}\psi_{*}}\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
induced by the
\sigma
-dual homomorphism
{}^{L}\psi\colon{}^{L}H\rightarrow{}^{L}G
. In other words, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})}(\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}(\mathrm{H}^{*}(\Pi))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\psi_{*}}

Example 9.1.2

(Treumann-Venkatesh functoriality conjecture)
.

If
\Pi
is an irreducible smooth representation of
G(E)
, then
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
acts on
\Pi
through a character
\chi_{\Pi}
. The composition
\chi_{\Pi}\circ F
is the character of
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
associated to the Frobenius twist
\Pi^{(\ell)}:=\Pi\otimes_{k,\Frob_{\ell}}k
, so we have

F_{*}\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}(\Pi)=\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}(F_{*}\Pi)=\{\chi_{\Pi^{(\ell)}}\}.

Suppose furthermore that
\Pi\cong{}^{\sigma}\Pi\in\Rep_{k}^{\sm}G(E)
. Then by
[
TV16
, Proposition 6.1]
, the
G(E)
-action on
\Pi
extends uniquely to a
G(E)\rtimes\Sigma
-action. Then Theorem
9.1.1
implies, assuming
\ell>\max\{b(\widehat{G}),b(\widehat{H})\}
, that for each
j\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
and each irreducible
H(E)
-subquotient
\pi
of
\mathrm{T}^{i}(\Pi)
, the semi-simple
L
-parameter
\rho_{\Pi^{(\ell)}}^{\ss}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{G}(k))
is the image of the semi-simple
L
-parameter
\rho_{\pi}
under the map
\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{H}(k))\xrightarrow{{}^{L}\psi_{*}}\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{G}(k))
. This implies Theorem
1.3.1
.

Example 9.1.3
.

Suppose
\sigma
is an
inner
automorphism, induced by conjugation by
s\in G(E)
(which therefore has to be an
\ell
-torsion element). Then we have an isomorphism
G(E)\rtimes\Sigma\xrightarrow{\sim}G(E)\times\Sigma
sending
(g,\sigma)\mapsto(gs^{-1},\sigma)
. Hence we get a functor

D^{b}(\Rep^{\sm}_{k}G(E))\rightarrow D^{b}(\Rep^{\sm}_{k}(G(E)\times\Sigma))\xrightarrow{\sim}D^{b}(\Rep^{\sm}_{k}(G(E)\rtimes\Sigma)).

This equips every representation
\Pi\in D^{b}(\Rep^{\sm}_{k}G(E))
with a canonical
\Sigma
-equivariant structure, where
\sigma
acts by
\Pi(s)
.

9.2.
The Treumann-Venkatesh homomorphism

We next establish some technical lemmas which aid to study the properties of the Brauer homomorphism.

Proposition 9.2.1
.

Let
K\subset G
be a
\Sigma
-stable compact open subgroup with prime-to-
\ell
pro-order. Then the natural map

\iota^{*}K\backslash H(E)/\iota^{*}K\rightarrow K\backslash G(E)/K

is injective.

Proof.

Let
a,b\in\iota^{*}K\backslash H(E)
be two elements whose images in
K\backslash G(E)
lie in the same orbit for the right translation of
K
. In other words,
a=b\kappa
for some
\kappa\in K
. Applying
\sigma
to this equation, and using that
a,b
are fixed by
\sigma
, we obtain
a=b\sigma(\kappa)
, so
\sigma(\kappa)\kappa^{-1}\in\Stab_{K}(b)
. Since
\Sigma
is of order
\ell
while
K
is prime-to-
\ell
, we have
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Sigma;\Stab_{K}(b))=0
. Hence there exists
y\in\Stab_{K}(b)
such that
\sigma(\kappa)\kappa^{-1}=\sigma(y)y^{-1}
. Then
y^{-1}\kappa
is fixed by
\sigma
, so
y^{-1}\kappa\in H(E)\cap K=\iota^{*}K
. But then

a=b\kappa=(by^{-1})\kappa=b(y^{-1}\kappa),

which shows that
a
and
b
lie in the same orbit for the right translation of
\iota^{*}K
on
\iota^{*}K\backslash H(E)
.
∎

Recall that we defined a compact open subgroup
K\subset G(E)
to be
plain
if the natural map
H(E)/\iota^{*}K\rightarrow[G(E)/K]^{\sigma}

is an isomorphism. By a similar argument involving the vanishing of non-abelian cohomology (cf.
[
Fen24
, Lemma 6.6]
), any prime-to-
\ell
subgroup
K\subset G(E)
is plain, so the (un-normalized) Brauer homomorphism (cf. §
7.1.1
)
\Br\colon\mathscr{H}(G,K)^{\sigma}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}(H,\iota^{*}K)
is defined for any such
K
.

Corollary 9.2.2
.

Let
K\subset G
be a
\Sigma
-stable prime-to-
\ell
compact open subgroup. Then
\Br
induces a map of centers,

(9.1)

\Br\colon Z(\mathscr{H}(G,K)^{\sigma})\rightarrow Z(\mathscr{H}(H,\iota^{*}K)).

Proof.

Proposition
9.2.1
implies that
\Br\colon\mathscr{H}(G,K)^{\sigma}\rightarrow\mathscr{H}(H,\iota^{*}K)
is a surjective
k
-algebra homomorphism, so it maps the center to the center.
∎

It is evident from the definition that the map (
9.1
) factors over the quotient
Z(\mathscr{H}(G,K)^{\sigma})/N\cdot\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)
, which induces a map

(9.2)

\mathrm{T}^{0}\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)=\frac{\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)^{\sigma}}{N\cdot\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)}\hookrightarrow\frac{Z(\mathscr{H}(G,K)^{\sigma})}{N\cdot\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Z}(H,\iota^{*}K).

Note that we have a natural
\mathbf{F}_{\ell}
-structure on
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K;k)
given by

(9.3)

\mathfrak{Z}(G,K;k)=\mathfrak{Z}(G,K;\mathbf{F}_{\ell})\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{\ell}}k.

The following is a “normalized” (cf. §
7.1.3
) version of (
9.1
), in the sense of the normalized Brauer homomorphism (
7.4
).

Definition 9.2.3
.

Let
K\subset G
be a
\Sigma
-stable prime-to-
\ell
compact open subgroup. We define
Treumann-Venkatesh homomorphism

\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV,K}\colon\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)\rightarrow\mathfrak{Z}(H,\iota^{*}K)

to be the composition of (
9.2
) with the map
\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\colon\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{0}(\mathfrak{Z}(G,K))
from Definition
7.1.1
, with respect to the
\mathbf{F}_{\ell}
-structure (
9.3
).

9.3.
Modular functoriality for Bernstein centers

For an inclusion
K\subset K^{\prime}\subset G(E)
of
\Sigma
-stable prime-to-
\ell
compact open subgroups, we have the map
e_{G}^{K\rightarrow K^{\prime}}\colon\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)\rightarrow\mathfrak{Z}(G,K^{\prime})
given by convolution with
\mathbbm{1}_{K^{\prime}}
. Similarly, we have
e_{H}^{K\rightarrow K^{\prime}}\colon\mathfrak{Z}(H,\iota^{*}K)\rightarrow\mathfrak{Z}(H,\iota^{*}K^{\prime})
given by convolution with
\mathbbm{1}_{\iota^{*}K^{\prime}}
. The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}(H,\iota^{*}K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}(G,K^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}(H,\iota^{*}K^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV,K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{G}^{K\rightarrow K^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{H}^{K\rightarrow K^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV,K^{\prime}}}

commutes.

Definition 9.3.1

(Base change homomorphism for Bernstein centers)
.

We define the
Treumann-Venkatesh homomorphism
(for Bernstein centers)
\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV}\colon\mathfrak{Z}(G)\rightarrow\mathfrak{Z}(H)
as

\varprojlim_{K}\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV,K}\colon\varprojlim_{K}\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)\rightarrow\varprojlim_{K}\mathfrak{Z}(H,\iota^{*}K)

where the limit is taken over
\Sigma
-stable prime-to-
\ell
compact open subgroups
K\subset G(E)
.

Assume
\ell>\max\{b(\widehat{G}),b(\widehat{H})\}
. Recall that in Corollary
7.6.2
we have constructed the
\sigma
-dual homomorphism
{}^{L}\psi\colon{}^{L}H\rightarrow{}^{L}G
over
k
.

Theorem 9.3.2
.

Assume
\ell>\max\{b(\widehat{G}),b(\widehat{H})\}
. Then the following diagram commutes:

(9.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}(H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\psi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FS_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FS_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV}}

Note that if
K
is a plain subgroup of
G(E)
, then we have

(9.5)

\mathrm{T}^{0}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)\cong\cInd_{\iota^{*}K}^{H(E)}k\in\Rep_{k}^{\mathrm{sm}}H(E).

(This is a special case of
[
BFH+
, Proposition 8.12]
; more general results on the interaction of Tate cohomology with compact induction are discussed later in §
10.4
.)

In preparation for the proof of Theorem
9.3.2
, we record the following interpretation of the Brauer homomorphism.

Lemma 9.3.3
.

Under the identifications
\mathscr{H}(G,K)\cong\End_{G(E)}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)
and
\mathscr{H}(H,\iota^{*}K)\cong\End_{H(E)}(\cInd_{\iota^{*}K}^{H(E)}k)
, the map
\Br\colon\mathrm{T}^{0}\mathscr{H}(G,K)\rightarrow\mathscr{H}(H,\iota^{*}K)
induced by the Brauer homomorphism is identified with the map

\mathrm{T}^{0}\End_{G(E)}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)\rightarrow\End_{H(E)}(\mathrm{T}^{0}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq: tate of cInd}}}{{\cong}}\End_{H(E)}(\cInd_{\iota^{*}K}^{H(E)}k)

sending a
\Sigma
-equivariant endomorphism of
\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k
to the induced endomorphism on its Tate cohomology.

Proof.

Apply
[
Fen24
, Lemma 6.7]
(which comes from
[
TV16
, §6.2]
) with
\Pi:=\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.3.2
.

For any prime-to-
\ell
,
\Sigma
-stable compact open subgroup
K\subset G(E)
, we also denote by

\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\xrightarrow{\FS_{G}}\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)

the composition of
\FS_{G}
with the projection
\mathfrak{Z}(G)\rightarrow\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)
. Applying Tate cohomology, this induces

(9.6)

\mathrm{T}^{0}\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\xrightarrow{\FS_{G}}\mathrm{T}^{0}\mathfrak{Z}(G,K).

We have identifications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{0}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(G,1_{G},\{0\}),K};\mathbbm{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(1_{G})}\mathrm{T}^{0}(\Sht_{(H,1_{H},I),\iota^{*}K}^{b^{\prime}};\mathbbm{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cInd_{\iota^{*}K}^{H(E)}k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{0}(\cInd_{\iota^{*}K}^{H(E)}k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
Ex.
8.5.1
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
(
8.14
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
Ex.
8.5.1
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
Ex.
3.7.4

and for any excursion datum
\mathcal{D}
for
\widehat{G}
, Theorem
8.6.2
implies that these identifications intertwine

(9.7)

\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\text{the action of }\text{$S_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\mathcal{D}}$}\\
\text{on $\mathrm{T}^{0}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)$}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\text{the action of $S_{{}^{L}\psi^{*}\mathcal{D}}$}\\
\text{on $\cInd_{\iota^{*}K}^{H(E)}k$}\end{array}\right).

The diagram

(9.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{0}\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}^{0}\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FS_{G}}
(
9.6
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}}

commutes by the definition of
\Nm
, and the fact that
\FS_{G}
is defined over
\mathbf{F}_{\ell}
(with respect to the
\mathbf{F}_{\ell}
-structures used to linearize
\Nm
on each row). Therefore, unraveling the definition of
\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV}
and using Lemma
8.5.5
, it suffices to show that

(9.9)

\Br(\FS_{G}(S_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\mathcal{D}}))=\FS_{H}(S_{{}^{L}\psi^{*}\mathcal{D}})

for all excursion data
\mathcal{D}
for
\widehat{G}
. By Lemma
9.3.3
,
\Br(\FS_{G}(S_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\mathcal{D}}))
is the endomorphism of
\mathrm{T}^{0}(\cInd_{\iota^{*}K}^{H(E)}k)=\cInd_{\iota^{*}K}^{H(E)}k
obtained by taking
\mathrm{T}^{0}
of the action of
S_{\Nm^{(\ell^{-1})}\mathcal{D}}
on
\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k
, which according to (
9.7
) is the endomorphism given by
\FS_{H}(S_{{}^{L}\psi^{*}\mathcal{D}})
.
∎

Corollary 9.3.4
.

Assume
\ell>\max\{b(\widehat{G}),b(\widehat{H})\}
. Then for any irreducible
H(E)
-representation
\pi
, the character

\chi_{\pi}\circ\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV}\colon\mathfrak{Z}(G)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV}}\mathfrak{Z}(H)\xrightarrow{\chi_{\pi}}k

has the property that for any irreducible
G(E)
-representation
\Pi
on which
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
acts through
\chi_{\pi}\circ\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV}
, there is an isomorphism of semi-simple
L
-parameters
\rho_{\Pi}\cong{}^{L}\psi\circ\rho_{\pi}
.

9.4.
Proof of Theorem
9.1.1

If
\ell>\max\{b(\widehat{G}),b(\widehat{H})\}
, it was seen in the proof of Theorem
9.3.2
that for any
\Sigma
-stable open compact subgroup
K<G(E)
, the normed action (cf. §
8.5.3
) of
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
on
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\cInd_{K}^{G(E)}k)
coincides under the identification (
9.5
) with the action obtained by composing
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\xrightarrow{\FS_{G}}\mathfrak{Z}(G)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV}}\mathfrak{Z}(H)
with the tautological action of
\mathfrak{Z}(H)
on
\cInd_{\iota^{*}K}^{H(E)}k
. Therefore, by Theorem
9.3.2
, for any
j
the map
{}^{L}\psi_{*}\colon\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})\rightarrow\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
carries

(9.10)

\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})}^{\mathrm{native}}\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi)\xrightarrow{{}^{L}\psi_{*}}\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}^{\mathrm{normed}}\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi),

where the native excursion action is defined in §
8.5.4
.

We will compare the support of the normed and native actions of
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}
on
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi)
. By the discussion in §
8.5.3
, we have for any
j\in\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
an equality

(9.11)

\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}}^{\mathrm{normed}}(\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi))=F_{*}\supp^{\mathrm{native}}_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}}(\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi))\subset\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}.

By the Tate spectral sequence
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\mathrm{H}^{i}(\Pi))\implies\mathrm{T}^{i+j}(\Pi)
, each
\mathrm{T}^{n}(\Pi)
has a finite filtration each of whose graded pieces is a subquotient of
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\Pi)
, hence we have

(9.12)

F_{*}\supp^{\mathrm{native}}_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}}(\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi))\subset F_{*}\supp^{\mathrm{native}}_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}}(\mathrm{H}^{*}(\Pi))=\supp^{\mathrm{native}}_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}}(F_{*}\mathrm{H}^{*}(\Pi)).

Let
q\colon\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\rightarrow\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}
be the map of spectra induced by the obvious inclusion. Combining (
9.11
) and (
9.12
) with (
9.10
) yields

(9.13)

q\circ{}^{L}\psi_{*}\left(\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})}^{\mathrm{native}}\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi)\right)\subset F_{*}\supp^{\mathrm{native}}_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}}(\mathrm{H}^{*}(\Pi)).

The surjection
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{T}^{0}\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
induces a closed embedding on spectra, as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\mathrm{T}^{0}\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Now,
[
Fen24
, Lemma 5.15]
says that any character from
\mathrm{T}^{0}\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
to any perfect field has a unique extension to
\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
, which implies that the map
\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\rightarrow\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}
is one-to-one (and surjective) over the closed subscheme
\Spec\mathrm{T}^{0}\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})\subset\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})^{\sigma}
. From Theorem
8.6.2
, we see that
q\circ{}^{L}\psi_{*}\left(\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})}^{\mathrm{native}}\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi)\right)
lands inside this subspace, so we may lift (
9.13
) to the containment

(9.14)

{}^{L}\psi_{*}\left(\supp_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})}^{\mathrm{native}}\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi)\right)\subset F_{*}\supp^{\mathrm{native}}_{\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}(\Pi),

as desired.

∎

9.5.
Existence of functorial lifts

We generalize the proof of
[
Fen24
, Theorem 6.26]
to prove the existence of functorial liftings along any
\sigma
-dual homomorphism.

Theorem 9.5.1
.

Assume
\ell>\max\{b(\widehat{G}),b(\widehat{H})\}
. Let
\pi
be an irreducible representation of
H(E)
over
k
, with Fargues-Scholze parameter
\rho_{\pi}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{H}(k))
. Then there is an irreducible representation
\Pi
of
G(E)
over
k
such that
\rho_{\Pi}\cong{}^{L}\psi\circ\rho_{\pi}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{G}(k))
.

Proof.

Choose a
\Sigma
-stable pro-
p
compact open subgroup
K<G(E)
such that
\pi^{\iota^{*}K}\neq 0
. From Theorem
9.3.2
we have a commutative diagram

(9.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\mathfrak{Z}(H,\iota^{*}K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\mathfrak{Z}_{\TV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\FS_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\FS_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\psi_{*}}

The representation
\pi
gives a
k
-point of
\Spec\mathfrak{Z}(H,\iota^{*}K)
lying over
\rho_{\pi}
viewed as a
k
-point of
\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{H})
. The commutativity of the diagram then tells us that there is a
k
-point of
\Spec\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)
, say with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
, lying over the
k
-point of
\Spec\Exc_{k}(W_{E},\widehat{G})
corresponding to
{}^{L}\psi\circ\rho_{\pi}
.

Recall that the functor
\Pi\mapsto\Pi^{K}
induces a bijection between irreducible admissible
G(E)
-representations with non-zero
K
-invariants and irreducible
\mathscr{H}(G,K)
-modules. It therefore suffices to construct an irreducible representation of
\mathscr{H}(G,K)
on which the
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)
-action factors over
\mathfrak{m}
.

By a result of Dat-Helm-Kurinczuk-Moss
[
DHKM24
, Theorem 1.1]

\mathscr{H}(G,K)
is a finite
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)
-module. By the Artin-Tate Lemma,
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)
is finite over
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)^{\sigma}
and then
\mathscr{H}(G,K)
is finite over
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)^{\sigma}
. The localization of
\mathscr{H}(G,K)
at
\mathfrak{m}
is non-zero, since
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{H}(G,K)
, so Nakayama’s Lemma implies that the left
\mathscr{H}(G,K)
-module quotient
\mathscr{H}(G,K)/\mathscr{H}(G,K)\mathfrak{m}
is finite-dimensional and non-zero. By design, it is supported over
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)/\mathfrak{m}
, so it has an irreducible
\mathscr{H}(G,K)
-subquotient on which the
\mathfrak{Z}(G,K)
-action factors over
\mathfrak{m}
, as was to be showed.

∎

10.
Fargues-Scholze parameters of toral supercuspidals

In this section we will demonstrate how the preceding theory may be used to calculate the Fargues-Scholze parameters attached to explicitly constructed representations
\Pi\in D^{b}(\Rep^{\mathrm{sm}}_{k}G(E))
. We will apply Theorem
9.1.1
with the automorphism
\sigma
being conjugation by a strongly regular
\ell
-torsion element
s\in G(E)
. Then
H
is a
torus

T<G
, so the Fargues-Scholze correspondence is completely understood for
H
. Using Theorem
9.1.1
, we can describe
\rho_{\Pi}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\widehat{G}(k))
in terms of
\mathrm{T}^{j}(\Pi)
and the
\sigma
-dual homomorphism
{}^{L}\psi
.

We will focus our attention on
\Pi\in D^{b}(\Rep^{\mathrm{sm}}_{k}G(E))
related to the “(Howe-unramified) toral supercuspidals” studied by Chan-Oi
[
CO
]
. (The method has broader scope but we regard this as a sufficiently interesting proof-of-concept for now.) Then
T
must be taken to be an unramified elliptic torus, and we compute the
\sigma
-dual embedding in §
10.2
: it turns out to be the canonical
L
-embedding corresponding to an unramified maximal torus.

The relevant
\Pi
arise as compact inductions of representations cut out from the cohomology of so-called “deep level Deligne-Lusztig varieties” studied by Chan-Ivanov
[
CI21
]
. In §
10.3
we use equivariant localization to calculate the Tate cohomology of these deep level Deligne-Lusztig varieties. We feed the answer into §
10.4
in order to compute the Tate cohomology of the compact inductions, and then tie together the calculations to describe the Fargues-Scholze parameters explicitly in Theorem
10.4.1
.

10.1.
Review of tori

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
E
.

10.1.1.
The abstract Cartan

To
G
we can associate a canonical
E
-torus
\mathbf{T}
, which we call the “abstract Cartan” of
G
. (We make no claim that
\mathbf{T}
admits an
E
-rational embedding into
G
.) If
G
is quasi-split, thne
\mathbf{T}
is defined as the colimit over
E
-rational Borel subgroups
B<G
of
B/U
, where
U
is the unipotent radical of
B
. In general, we pass to an extension of
G
where it becomes quasisplit, and then descend this construction.

Given an extension
E^{\prime}/E
and a Borus
(B,T)
over
G_{E^{\prime}}
, the composition

T\hookrightarrow B\twoheadrightarrow B/U\rightarrow\mathbf{T}_{E^{\prime}}

defines an isomorphism
i_{B}\colon T\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{T}_{E^{\prime}}
.

We denote by
\mathbf{W}
the Weyl group of
\mathbf{T}
, defined as the colimit of
N_{G_{E^{s}}}(T)/T
over the category of Bori
(B,T)
in
G_{E^{s}}
.

10.1.2.
The cocycle of a torus

Let
T\subset G
be a maximal torus over
E
. Over
E^{s}
, we can find a Borus
(B,T_{E^{s}})
inside
G_{E^{s}}
, which gives an identification
i_{B}\colon T_{E^{s}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{T}_{E^{s}}
.

For
\gamma\in\operatorname{Gal}(E^{s}/E)
,
\gamma B
is another Borel subgroup of
G
containing
T_{E^{s}}
, so we have another identification
i_{\gamma B}\colon T_{E^{s}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{T}_{E^{s}}
.

For
\gamma\in\operatorname{Gal}(E^{s}/E)
, we denote by
\gamma_{\mathbf{T}}
(resp.
\gamma_{T}
) the endomorphism of
\mathbf{T}_{E^{s}}
(resp.
T_{E^{s}}
) induced by
\gamma
. From the definition of the abstract Cartan, we see that
\gamma_{\mathbf{T}}
is the composition

\mathbf{T}_{E^{s}}\xrightarrow{i_{B}^{-1}}T_{E^{s}}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{T}}T_{E^{s}}\xrightarrow{i_{\gamma B}}\mathbf{T}_{E^{s}}.

For each
\gamma\in\operatorname{Gal}(E^{s}/E)
, the automorphism
i_{B}i_{\gamma B}^{-1}
of
\mathbf{T}_{E^{s}}
is given by an element of
\mathbf{W}
. Hence the function
z_{T,B}\colon\gamma\mapsto(i_{B}i_{\gamma B}^{-1})
defines a cocycle in
Z^{1}(\operatorname{Gal}(E^{s}/E);\mathbf{W})
. Choosing a different
B
alters
z_{T,B}
by a coboundary, hence the cohomology class
h_{T}:=[z_{T,B}]\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\operatorname{Gal}(E^{s}/E);\mathbf{W})
is independent of
B
. Then we have

i_{B}\gamma_{T}i_{B}^{-1}=i_{B}(i_{\gamma B}^{-1}\gamma_{\mathbf{T}}i_{B})i_{B}^{-1}=(i_{B}i_{\gamma B}^{-1})\gamma_{\mathbf{T}}.

In other words, the cocycle
h_{T}
expresses the “difference” between the
\operatorname{Gal}(E^{s}/E)
-action on
T
and on
\mathbf{T}
.

10.1.3.
The canonical
L
-embedding

Recall from the work of Langlands-Shelstad
[
LS87
]
that given a maximal torus
T\subset G
together with a choice of “
\chi
-data”, there is a
\widehat{G}
-conjugacy class of admissible dual embeddings
{}^{L}j\colon{}^{L}T\hookrightarrow{}^{L}G
. In general there is no distingushed choice of
{}^{L}j
, but if
T
is
unramified
then it has a distinguished choice of
\chi
-data, which gives a
canonical
conjugacy class of embeddings
{}^{L}j\colon{}^{L}T\rightarrow{}^{L}G
. We will describe it more explicitly.

Using the
\widehat{G}
-conjugation, we can arrange that
\widehat{j}
sends
\widehat{T}
isomorphically to
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}
. Then
{}^{L}j
is specified by a cocycle
W_{E}\rightarrow\widehat{G}(k)
, which must land in the normalizer of
\widehat{T}
in
\widehat{G}
, denoted
N_{\widehat{G}}(\widehat{T})(k)
. This cocycle will be chosen to lift
h_{T}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\mathbf{W})
. Since
T
is unramified, all the actions factor through the unramified quotient
\mathrm{val}\colon W_{E}\twoheadrightarrow\langle\varphi\rangle\cong\mathbf{Z}
, and our lift will be chosen to be inflated from
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\langle\varphi\rangle;N_{\widehat{G}}(\widehat{T})(k))
. The space of such liftings is controlled by an exact sequence

\mathrm{H}^{1}(\langle\varphi\rangle;\widehat{T}(k))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1}(\langle\varphi\rangle;N_{\widehat{G}}(\widehat{T})(k))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1}(\langle\varphi\rangle;\mathbf{W}).

We will explicate the correct lift only in the case that
T
is
elliptic
, i.e., anisotropic mod center.

First suppose
G
is semi-simple: then
T
is anisotropic, so that

\mathrm{H}^{1}(\langle\varphi\rangle;\widehat{T}(k))=\widehat{T}(k)/\varphi\mathrm{-conj}=\{1\},

hence
{}^{L}j
is uniquely determined in this case by the condition of lifting
h_{T}
.

In general, consider the adjoint quotient
G\twoheadrightarrow G_{\ad}
. Let
\overline{T}:=T/Z(G)
, so we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\ad}}

On the dual side we have
\widehat{G_{\ad}}\rightarrow\widehat{G}
and
\widehat{\overline{T}}\rightarrow\widehat{T}
, forming a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\overline{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G_{\ad}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}}

Let
{}^{L}j_{\ad}\colon{}^{L}\overline{T}\rightarrow{}^{L}G_{\ad}
be the
L
-embedding specified in the preceding paragraph.
Then from
[
DR09
, §4.3]
, one sees that
{}^{L}j
is the pushout of
{}^{L}j_{\ad}
, as in the following pushout square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\overline{T}}\rtimes W_{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{T}\rtimes W_{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G_{\ad}}\rtimes W_{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}\rtimes W_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}j_{\ad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}j}

10.2.
Calculation of the
\sigma
-dual homomorphism

Let
T\subset G
be an unramified elliptic maximal torus defined over
E
.

Proposition 10.2.1
.

Suppose
T(E)
contains an element
s
of order
\ell
which maps to a strongly regular element of
G_{\ad}(E)
. Let
\sigma=\mathrm{conj}_{s}
be the conjugation action of
s
on
G
, so
G^{\sigma}=T
. Assume
\ell>b(\check{G})
. Then the
\sigma
-dual homomorphism
{}^{L}\psi\colon{}^{L}T\rightarrow{}^{L}G
lies in the
\widehat{G}
-conjugacy class of the composition

{}^{L}T\xrightarrow{\Fr_{\ell}}{}^{L}T\xrightarrow{{}^{L}j}{}^{L}G.

where
\Fr_{\ell}
is induced by the Frobenius endomorphism
\check{T}\rightarrow\check{T}
(corresponding to multiplication by
\ell
on character groups).

We begin with some preliminaries before turning to the proof. Any choice of
B\supset T_{E^{s}}
gives a commutative diagram

(10.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{E^{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{E^{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{E^{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}_{E^{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{E^{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{B}}

By Lemma
7.5.2
and Corollary
7.6.7
, diagram (
10.1
) induces an isomorphism

(10.2)

br\cong(\Fr_{\ell})^{*}i_{B}^{*}\CT_{B}\colon\Rep_{k}(\widehat{G})\rightarrow\Rep_{k}(\widehat{T}).

Thus the choice of
B
gives an identification
\widehat{i}_{B}^{-1}\colon\widehat{T}\xrightarrow{\sim}\widehat{\mathbf{T}}
in such a way that the map
\check{\psi}
, the restriction of
{}^{L}\psi
to the identity component, factors as in the diagram below

(10.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\phi}_{B}}

where
\check{\phi}_{B}
is
\widehat{i}_{B}^{-1}
composed with the Frobenius endomorphism
\Fr_{\ell}
of
\widehat{T}
. As discussed in §
10.1.2
, the map
\widehat{i}_{B}
carries the
W_{E}
-action on
\check{T}
to the
W_{E}
-action on
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}
twisted by the cocycle
h_{T}
, so for all
\gamma\in W_{E}
we have

(10.4)

\check{\psi}\circ\gamma_{\widehat{T}}=h_{T}(\gamma)\cdot(\gamma_{\widehat{\mathbf{T}}}\circ\check{\phi}_{B}).

This has the following consequence. The map (
10.2
) carries
W_{E}\subset{}^{L}T
to
N_{\widehat{G}}(\widehat{\mathbf{T}})\rtimes W_{E}
, so its projection to the first component defines a class
\pr_{1}({}^{L}\psi)\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};N_{\widehat{G}}(\widehat{\mathbf{T}}))
. Denote by
[\pr_{1}({}^{L}\psi)]\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\mathbf{W})
the projection of
\pr_{1}({}^{L}\psi)
along
N_{\check{G}}(\check{\mathbf{T}})\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{W}
. From (
10.4
) we conclude:

Lemma 10.2.2
.

We have
h_{T}=[\pr_{1}({}^{L}\psi)]\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(W_{E};\mathbf{W})
.

As explained in §
10.1.3
, Lemma
10.2.2
already shows that Proposition
10.2.1
holds if
G
is semi-simple and
T
is an elliptic unramified torus. For the general case, note that by hypothesis
s
maps to a strongly regular element
s\in G_{\ad}
of order
\ell
, whose centralizer is
\overline{T}
, so a similar theory applies to
G_{\ad}
. This induces a functor

\widetilde{br}_{\ad}\colon\Sat(\Gr_{G_{\ad},\Div_{X}^{1}};k)\rightarrow\Sat(\Gr_{\overline{T},\Div_{X}^{1}};k)

and we study its relation to the functor

\widetilde{br}\colon\Sat(\Gr_{G,\Div_{X}^{1}};k)\rightarrow\Sat(\Gr_{T,\Div_{X}^{1}};k)

which corresponds under Geometric Satake to
{}^{L}\psi
.
